    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. Like I don’t really know how to just sit still or idle, if that makes sense. I’m not saying it’s some huge dramatic thing every second, but it’s there enough that I notice it.\n\nAnd with the way work is set up now, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover for it. I’m a customer service rep, so I’m used to having a certain rhythm, you know—calls, little tasks, switching around—and I could sort of blend it in before. But now the setup makes it more obvious to me that I’m constantly needing to shift, get up, do something, check something, move on to the next thing. It’s like I can’t just settle into one gear and stay there.\n\nSo I figured I should come in and talk to somebody, because it’s starting to feel like I’m working harder to manage that than I probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, honestly. I mean, I can be quick to respond sometimes, like if something comes up I might jump into handling it before I’ve fully thought through every angle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s, you know, reckless or constantly making big spur-of-the-moment decisions. It’s more like little everyday things, maybe—I’ll start doing something because it feels like it needs to be done right then, and only afterward I’ll think, “Okay, maybe I could’ve paused for a second.” But I haven’t really tracked it in my head as a main issue, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do, especially if it’s something that kind of stays in one lane for too long. Like I can start out okay — I’ll be listening, or reading, and I’m thinking, “All right, I’m with this.” But then after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the words or looking at the person talking, but I didn’t really absorb what they said.\n\nIt happens in longer coaching meetings at work, for example. If someone’s going over feedback or a new way we’re supposed to handle calls, I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then my eyes are still moving like I’m following along, but my mind has sort of slipped off somewhere else. Same thing when I’m reading a new procedure. I’ll get through a section and then have to go back because I’m like, “Wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it. I’m trying to focus, and at the beginning I usually can. It’s more that keeping it there for a longer stretch feels harder than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not in a way where I’m, like, jumping out of my chair every two seconds, but I definitely feel it when I’m supposed to just sit there and be still.\n\nLike if there’s a training video at work and I’m just sitting there watching it, especially if it’s slow or kind of drawn out, I start feeling sort of boxed in. That’s the best way I can describe it. My body starts wanting to do something — stand up, stretch, shift around, tap my fingers, mess with a pen, anything really. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I need to leave,” exactly, but sitting there without moving starts to feel uncomfortable.\n\nSame kind of thing with a slow appointment, like if I’m waiting or I have to sit through something where I’m not really doing much. I’ll be trying to stay polite and sit still, but inside I’m very aware of wanting to get up or at least move my hands or adjust in the chair. It’s mild, I guess, but it’s there enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one, because I don’t really think of myself as having a big problem with organizing or time management. I mean, I’ve had normal moments where I underestimate how long something will take, or I’ll have to remind myself what step comes next, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people deal with.\n\nAt work, I usually just try to go by whatever the process is or whatever needs to be handled next, so there’s some structure already there. If you mean like planning out a whole day from scratch or keeping track of a bunch of separate things, I guess I can get a little scattered sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a major issue.\n\nSo… maybe mildly at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I “often” have trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anger, excitement, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” at least not in a way that feels out of the ordinary to me. I mean, I definitely get frustrated sometimes — like anybody does — especially if something is dragging on or if I feel like I’m not getting what I need from a situation. But I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly having these big emotional swings or blowing up.\n\nWith anger, I usually try to keep it contained. I might feel irritated inside, and maybe I get quieter or a little short, but I don’t think I’m regularly losing my temper. Excitement… I mean, sure, I can get excited about things, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or hard to control.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I have emotions, obviously, and I can get frustrated, but I haven’t really noticed strong emotions being a major problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that some, especially in work situations. Like in a team huddle, if I’m waiting for my turn to speak or waiting for them to get around to whatever part involves me, I can get kind of antsy. I’ll be standing there or sitting there trying to look like I’m just listening normally, but inside I’m sort of waiting, waiting, waiting, and I start wanting to jump in before it’s really my turn.\n\nSame thing with the help queue at work. If I’m waiting for the next thing to come through, or waiting for something to be assigned, I catch myself checking the screen over and over. Like I know it’s not going to make it move faster, but I keep looking anyway. It’s not like I’m making a scene or anything, but I can feel myself getting impatient and wanting to just do something or get to my part already.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say I have some trouble with waiting my turn, but it’s more like I get restless and impatient internally than anything dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do that sometimes. I don’t usually mean it as interrupting, exactly, but I can see how it probably comes across that way.\n\nAt work, if I’m near coworkers and I hear them talking through a customer issue — like they’re trying to figure out what to tell someone or where to find something in the system — I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’re done explaining it, or before anyone actually asked me. In my head it’s like, “Oh, I know what that is,” and I just say it because I’m trying to help and move things along. But then afterward I realize, okay, maybe they were still working through it, or maybe the person wasn’t talking to me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to take over or be rude. It’s more like the answer pops up and I blurt it out a little too quickly. I try to catch myself, but yeah, I’d say that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished speaking in social or group settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m doing it in a social way where I’m trying to dominate the conversation or anything like that, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished talking.\n\nLike, someone might be explaining what a customer is asking about, or another coworker is trying to figure out where something is in the system, and before they even get all the way through it I’ll say, “Oh, you need to do this,” or “That’s over here,” or whatever. Sometimes nobody actually asked me — I just hear enough to think I know the answer, and it comes out.\n\nI don’t mean to be rude. In my mind I’m being helpful and saving time, especially if I’ve dealt with the same issue before. But I can tell there are times where I probably should’ve waited and let them finish, because I’m jumping in a little too soon. So yes, I’d say I do blurt things out like that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your impulses in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m out of control or anything dramatic, but in conversation at work, I do have trouble stopping myself from jumping in sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk and I hear a couple coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll catch enough of what they’re saying and my brain kind of goes, “Oh, I know the answer.” And then before I really think about whether they asked me, or whether they’re even done explaining the issue, I’ll just say it. I’ll be like, “No, you have to go to this screen,” or “That customer needs this form,” or whatever it is.\n\nI don’t mean it in a rude way. In my head I’m trying to help and maybe save them time, especially if it’s something I’ve dealt with before. But I can tell afterward that maybe I cut into their conversation, or I answered before they were finished talking. Sometimes I’ll realize, “Okay, I probably should’ve waited another few seconds and let them get through it.”\n\nSo yeah, that’s probably the main way it shows up socially for me — more at work than anywhere else. It’s like the answer comes to me and I have a hard time holding it back long enough to see if it’s actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it was there even when I was a kid. I didn’t really think of it that way at the time, of course — I was just “not applying myself” or “rushing” or whatever they called it back then.\n\nIn grade school, I remember teachers writing comments like I was bright, but careless. That was a pretty common theme. Like I understood the work, but I’d make mistakes because I didn’t stay with it carefully, or I’d turn something in that looked rushed. And I know I got reminded to stay on task — stay with my work, stop drifting off, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd the talking part too… I had teachers say I talked out of turn. Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.

Like I can start out okay — I’ll be listening, or reading, and I’m thinking, “All right, I’m with this.” But then after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the words or looking at the person talking, but I didn’t really absorb what they said.\n\nIt happens in longer coaching meetings at work, for example. If someone’s going over feedback or a new way we’re supposed to handle calls, I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then my eyes are still moving like I’m following along, but my mind has sort of slipped off somewhere else. Same thing when I’m reading a new procedure. I’ll get through a section and then have to go back because I’m like, “Wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it. I’m trying to focus, and at the beginning I usually can. It’s more that keeping it there for a longer stretch feels harder than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not in a way where I’m, like, jumping out of my chair every two seconds, but I definitely feel it when I’m supposed to just sit there and be still.\n\nLike if there’s a training video at work and I’m just sitting there watching it, especially if it’s slow or kind of drawn out, I start feeling sort of boxed in. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I need to leave,” exactly, but sitting there without moving starts to feel uncomfortable.\n\nSame kind of thing with a slow appointment, like if I’m waiting or I have to sit through something where I’m not really doing much. I mean, I’ve had normal moments where I underestimate how long something will take, or I’ll have to remind myself what step comes next, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people deal with.\n\nAt work, I usually just try to go by whatever the process is or whatever needs to be handled next, so there’s some structure already there. If you mean like planning out a whole day from scratch or keeping track of a bunch of separate things, I guess I can get a little scattered sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a major issue.\n\nSo… maybe mildly at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I “often” have trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anger, excitement, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” at least not in a way that feels out of the ordinary to me. I might feel irritated inside, and maybe I get quieter or a little short, but I don’t think I’m regularly losing my temper. Excitement… I mean, sure, I can get excited about things, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or hard to control.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I have emotions, obviously, and I can get frustrated, but I haven’t really noticed strong emotions being a major problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that some, especially in work situations. Like in a team huddle, if I’m waiting for my turn to speak or waiting for them to get around to whatever part involves me, I can get kind of antsy. I’ll be standing there or sitting there trying to look like I’m just listening normally, but inside I’m sort of waiting, waiting, waiting, and I start wanting to jump in before it’s really my turn.\n\nSame thing with the help queue at work. If I’m waiting for the next thing to come through, or waiting for something to be assigned, I catch myself checking the screen over and over. Like I know it’s not going to make it move faster, but I keep looking anyway. It’s not like I’m making a scene or anything, but I can feel myself getting impatient and wanting to just do something or get to my part already.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say I have some trouble with waiting my turn, but it’s more like I get restless and impatient internally than anything dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do that sometimes. I don’t usually mean it as interrupting, exactly, but I can see how it probably comes across that way.\n\nAt work, if I’m near coworkers and I hear them talking through a customer issue — like they’re trying to figure out what to tell someone or where to find something in the system — I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’re done explaining it, or before anyone actually asked me. In my head it’s like, “Oh, I know what that is,” and I just say it because I’m trying to help and move things along. But then afterward I realize, okay, maybe they were still working through it, or maybe the person wasn’t talking to me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to take over or be rude. It’s more like the answer pops up and I blurt it out a little too quickly. I try to catch myself, but yeah, I’d say that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished speaking in social or group settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m doing it in a social way where I’m trying to dominate the conversation or anything like that, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished talking.\n\nLike, someone might be explaining what a customer is asking about, or another coworker is trying to figure out where something is in the system, and before they even get all the way through it I’ll say, “Oh, you need to do this,” or “That’s over here,” or whatever. Sometimes nobody actually asked me — I just hear enough to think I know the answer, and it comes out.\n\nI don’t mean to be rude. In my mind I’m being helpful and saving time, especially if I’ve dealt with the same issue before. But I can tell there are times where I probably should’ve waited and let them finish, because I’m jumping in a little too soon. So yes, I’d say I do blurt things out like that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your impulses in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m out of control or anything dramatic, but in conversation at work, I do have trouble stopping myself from jumping in sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk and I hear a couple coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll catch enough of what they’re saying and my brain kind of goes, “Oh, I know the answer.” And then before I really think about whether they asked me, or whether they’re even done explaining the issue, I’ll just say it. I’ll be like, “No, you have to go to this screen,” or “That customer needs this form,” or whatever it is.\n\nI don’t mean it in a rude way. In my head I’m trying to help and maybe save them time, especially if it’s something I’ve dealt with before. But I can tell afterward that maybe I cut into their conversation, or I answered before they were finished talking. Sometimes I’ll realize, “Okay, I probably should’ve waited another few seconds and let them get through it.”\n\nSo yeah, that’s probably the main way it shows up socially for me — more at work than anywhere else. It’s like the answer comes to me and I have a hard time holding it back long enough to see if it’s actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it was there even when I was a kid. I didn’t really think of it that way at the time, of course — I was just “not applying myself” or “rushing” or whatever they called it back then.\n\nIn grade school, I remember teachers writing comments like I was bright, but careless. That was a pretty common theme. Like I understood the work, but I’d make mistakes because I didn’t stay with it carefully, or I’d turn something in that looked rushed. And I know I got reminded to stay on task — stay with my work, stop drifting off, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd the talking part too… I had teachers say I talked out of turn. Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Did you often engage in physical activities like running or playing sports as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.\n\nI wasn’t, like, the kid with a whole list of organized sports I was serious about, at least not that I remember. And I can’t really say, “Yes, I was always doing physical activities,” because I don’t want to overstate it. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Did you often engage in physical activities like running or playing sports as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.\n\nI wasn’t, like, the kid with a whole list of organized sports I was serious about, at least not that I remember. And I can’t really say, “Yes, I was always doing physical activities,” because I don’t want to overstate it. I remember more of the school comments and the classroom stuff than I remember whether I was unusually active outside.\n\nSo… maybe some normal running around and playing, but I don’t know that I can say it was often or excessive.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms ever caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so in a major way. I mean, I’m sure people close to me have probably gotten annoyed with me at times — everybody does things that irritate people — but I can’t point to, like, a relationship that fell apart because of this, or some big ongoing conflict that I’d say is clearly from my symptoms.

It’s easier for me to see the work side, because there are reviews and tickets and specific things people can point to. In my personal life it’s a little fuzzier. I generally get along with people okay, and I don’t feel like I’m constantly fighting with family or friends because of it.

So… I guess my answer would be no, not that I’ve really noticed. Maybe there are little irritations here and there, but nothing I’d call a significant relationship problem.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. Like I don’t really know how to just sit still or idle, if that makes sense. I’m not saying it’s some huge dramatic thing every second, but it’s there enough that I notice it.\n\nAnd with the way work is set up now, it’s gotten harder to kind of cover for it. I’m a customer service rep, so I’m used to having a certain rhythm, you know—calls, little tasks, switching around—and I could sort of blend it in before. But now the setup makes it more obvious to me that I’m constantly needing to shift, get up, do something, check something, move on to the next thing. It’s like I can’t just settle into one gear and stay there.\n\nSo I figured I should come in and talk to somebody, because it’s starting to feel like I’m working harder to manage that than I probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, honestly. I mean, I can be quick to respond sometimes, like if something comes up I might jump into handling it before I’ve fully thought through every angle, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by acting on impulse.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s, you know, reckless or constantly making big spur-of-the-moment decisions. It’s more like little everyday things, maybe—I’ll start doing something because it feels like it needs to be done right then, and only afterward I’ll think, “Okay, maybe I could’ve paused for a second.” But I haven’t really tracked it in my head as a main issue, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do, especially if it’s something that kind of stays in one lane for too long. Like I can start out okay — I’ll be listening, or reading, and I’m thinking, “All right, I’m with this.” But then after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the words or looking at the person talking, but I didn’t really absorb what they said.\n\nIt happens in longer coaching meetings at work, for example. If someone’s going over feedback or a new way we’re supposed to handle calls, I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then my eyes are still moving like I’m following along, but my mind has sort of slipped off somewhere else. Same thing when I’m reading a new procedure. I’ll get through a section and then have to go back because I’m like, “Wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it. I’m trying to focus, and at the beginning I usually can. It’s more that keeping it there for a longer stretch feels harder than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not in a way where I’m, like, jumping out of my chair every two seconds, but I definitely feel it when I’m supposed to just sit there and be still.\n\nLike if there’s a training video at work and I’m just sitting there watching it, especially if it’s slow or kind of drawn out, I start feeling sort of boxed in. That’s the best way I can describe it. My body starts wanting to do something — stand up, stretch, shift around, tap my fingers, mess with a pen, anything really. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I need to leave,” exactly, but sitting there without moving starts to feel uncomfortable.\n\nSame kind of thing with a slow appointment, like if I’m waiting or I have to sit through something where I’m not really doing much. I’ll be trying to stay polite and sit still, but inside I’m very aware of wanting to get up or at least move my hands or adjust in the chair. It’s mild, I guess, but it’s there enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one, because I don’t really think of myself as having a big problem with organizing or time management. I mean, I’ve had normal moments where I underestimate how long something will take, or I’ll have to remind myself what step comes next, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people deal with.\n\nAt work, I usually just try to go by whatever the process is or whatever needs to be handled next, so there’s some structure already there. If you mean like planning out a whole day from scratch or keeping track of a bunch of separate things, I guess I can get a little scattered sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a major issue.\n\nSo… maybe mildly at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I “often” have trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anger, excitement, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” at least not in a way that feels out of the ordinary to me. I mean, I definitely get frustrated sometimes — like anybody does — especially if something is dragging on or if I feel like I’m not getting what I need from a situation. But I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly having these big emotional swings or blowing up.\n\nWith anger, I usually try to keep it contained. I might feel irritated inside, and maybe I get quieter or a little short, but I don’t think I’m regularly losing my temper. Excitement… I mean, sure, I can get excited about things, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or hard to control.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I have emotions, obviously, and I can get frustrated, but I haven’t really noticed strong emotions being a major problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that some, especially in work situations. Like in a team huddle, if I’m waiting for my turn to speak or waiting for them to get around to whatever part involves me, I can get kind of antsy. I’ll be standing there or sitting there trying to look like I’m just listening normally, but inside I’m sort of waiting, waiting, waiting, and I start wanting to jump in before it’s really my turn.\n\nSame thing with the help queue at work. If I’m waiting for the next thing to come through, or waiting for something to be assigned, I catch myself checking the screen over and over. Like I know it’s not going to make it move faster, but I keep looking anyway. It’s not like I’m making a scene or anything, but I can feel myself getting impatient and wanting to just do something or get to my part already.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say I have some trouble with waiting my turn, but it’s more like I get restless and impatient internally than anything dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do that sometimes. I don’t usually mean it as interrupting, exactly, but I can see how it probably comes across that way.\n\nAt work, if I’m near coworkers and I hear them talking through a customer issue — like they’re trying to figure out what to tell someone or where to find something in the system — I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’re done explaining it, or before anyone actually asked me. In my head it’s like, “Oh, I know what that is,” and I just say it because I’m trying to help and move things along. But then afterward I realize, okay, maybe they were still working through it, or maybe the person wasn’t talking to me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to take over or be rude. It’s more like the answer pops up and I blurt it out a little too quickly. I try to catch myself, but yeah, I’d say that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished speaking in social or group settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m doing it in a social way where I’m trying to dominate the conversation or anything like that, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished talking.\n\nLike, someone might be explaining what a customer is asking about, or another coworker is trying to figure out where something is in the system, and before they even get all the way through it I’ll say, “Oh, you need to do this,” or “That’s over here,” or whatever. Sometimes nobody actually asked me — I just hear enough to think I know the answer, and it comes out.\n\nI don’t mean to be rude. In my mind I’m being helpful and saving time, especially if I’ve dealt with the same issue before. But I can tell there are times where I probably should’ve waited and let them finish, because I’m jumping in a little too soon. So yes, I’d say I do blurt things out like that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your impulses in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m out of control or anything dramatic, but in conversation at work, I do have trouble stopping myself from jumping in sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk and I hear a couple coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll catch enough of what they’re saying and my brain kind of goes, “Oh, I know the answer.” And then before I really think about whether they asked me, or whether they’re even done explaining the issue, I’ll just say it. I’ll be like, “No, you have to go to this screen,” or “That customer needs this form,” or whatever it is.\n\nI don’t mean it in a rude way. In my head I’m trying to help and maybe save them time, especially if it’s something I’ve dealt with before. But I can tell afterward that maybe I cut into their conversation, or I answered before they were finished talking. Sometimes I’ll realize, “Okay, I probably should’ve waited another few seconds and let them get through it.”\n\nSo yeah, that’s probably the main way it shows up socially for me — more at work than anywhere else. It’s like the answer comes to me and I have a hard time holding it back long enough to see if it’s actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it was there even when I was a kid. I didn’t really think of it that way at the time, of course — I was just “not applying myself” or “rushing” or whatever they called it back then.\n\nIn grade school, I remember teachers writing comments like I was bright, but careless. That was a pretty common theme. Like I understood the work, but I’d make mistakes because I didn’t stay with it carefully, or I’d turn something in that looked rushed. And I know I got reminded to stay on task — stay with my work, stop drifting off, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd the talking part too… I had teachers say I talked out of turn. Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Did you often engage in physical activities like running or playing sports as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.\n\nI wasn’t, like, the kid with a whole list of organized sports I was serious about, at least not that I remember. And I can’t really say, “Yes, I was always doing physical activities,” because I don’t want to overstate it. I remember more of the school comments and the classroom stuff than I remember whether I was unusually active outside.\n\nSo… maybe some normal running around and playing, but I don’t know that I can say it was often or excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in a major way. I mean, I’m sure people close to me have probably gotten annoyed with me at times — everybody does things that irritate people — but I can’t point to, like, a relationship that fell apart because of this, or some big ongoing conflict that I’d say is clearly from my symptoms.\n\nIt’s easier for me to see the work side, because there are reviews and tickets and specific things people can point to. I try to catch myself, but yeah, I’d say that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished speaking in social or group settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m doing it in a social way where I’m trying to dominate the conversation or anything like that, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished talking.\n\nLike, someone might be explaining what a customer is asking about, or another coworker is trying to figure out where something is in the system, and before they even get all the way through it I’ll say, “Oh, you need to do this,” or “That’s over here,” or whatever. Sometimes nobody actually asked me — I just hear enough to think I know the answer, and it comes out.\n\nI don’t mean to be rude. In my mind I’m being helpful and saving time, especially if I’ve dealt with the same issue before. But I can tell there are times where I probably should’ve waited and let them finish, because I’m jumping in a little too soon. So yes, I’d say I do blurt things out like that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your impulses in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m out of control or anything dramatic, but in conversation at work, I do have trouble stopping myself from jumping in sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk and I hear a couple coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll catch enough of what they’re saying and my brain kind of goes, “Oh, I know the answer.” And then before I really think about whether they asked me, or whether they’re even done explaining the issue, I’ll just say it. I’ll be like, “No, you have to go to this screen,” or “That customer needs this form,” or whatever it is.\n\nI don’t mean it in a rude way. In my head I’m trying to help and maybe save them time, especially if it’s something I’ve dealt with before. But I can tell afterward that maybe I cut into their conversation, or I answered before they were finished talking. Sometimes I’ll realize, “Okay, I probably should’ve waited another few seconds and let them get through it.”\n\nSo yeah, that’s probably the main way it shows up socially for me — more at work than anywhere else. It’s like the answer comes to me and I have a hard time holding it back long enough to see if it’s actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it was there even when I was a kid. I didn’t really think of it that way at the time, of course — I was just “not applying myself” or “rushing” or whatever they called it back then.\n\nIn grade school, I remember teachers writing comments like I was bright, but careless. That was a pretty common theme. Like I understood the work, but I’d make mistakes because I didn’t stay with it carefully, or I’d turn something in that looked rushed. And I know I got reminded to stay on task — stay with my work, stop drifting off, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd the talking part too… I had teachers say I talked out of turn. Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Did you often engage in physical activities like running or playing sports as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.\n\nI wasn’t, like, the kid with a whole list of organized sports I was serious about, at least not that I remember. And I can’t really say, “Yes, I was always doing physical activities,” because I don’t want to overstate it. I remember more of the school comments and the classroom stuff than I remember whether I was unusually active outside.\n\nSo… maybe some normal running around and playing, but I don’t know that I can say it was often or excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in a major way. I mean, I’m sure people close to me have probably gotten annoyed with me at times — everybody does things that irritate people — but I can’t point to, like, a relationship that fell apart because of this, or some big ongoing conflict that I’d say is clearly from my symptoms.\n\nIt’s easier for me to see the work side, because there are reviews and tickets and specific things people can point to. In my personal life it’s a little fuzzier. I generally get along with people okay, and I don’t feel like I’m constantly fighting with family or friends because of it.\n\nSo… I guess my answer would be no, not that I’ve really noticed. Maybe there are little irritations here and there, but nothing I’d call a significant relationship problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Yes, mostly at work. I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely rearranged my whole life around it, but I have had to change how I handle my day because it’s started showing up in my performance reviews.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. Like I can start out okay — I’ll be listening, or reading, and I’m thinking, “All right, I’m with this.” But then after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the words or looking at the person talking, but I didn’t really absorb what they said.\n\nIt happens in longer coaching meetings at work, for example. If someone’s going over feedback or a new way we’re supposed to handle calls, I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then my eyes are still moving like I’m following along, but my mind has sort of slipped off somewhere else. Same thing when I’m reading a new procedure. I’ll get through a section and then have to go back because I’m like, “Wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about it. I’m trying to focus, and at the beginning I usually can. It’s more that keeping it there for a longer stretch feels harder than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not in a way where I’m, like, jumping out of my chair every two seconds, but I definitely feel it when I’m supposed to just sit there and be still.\n\nLike if there’s a training video at work and I’m just sitting there watching it, especially if it’s slow or kind of drawn out, I start feeling sort of boxed in. That’s the best way I can describe it. My body starts wanting to do something — stand up, stretch, shift around, tap my fingers, mess with a pen, anything really. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I need to leave,” exactly, but sitting there without moving starts to feel uncomfortable.\n\nSame kind of thing with a slow appointment, like if I’m waiting or I have to sit through something where I’m not really doing much. I’ll be trying to stay polite and sit still, but inside I’m very aware of wanting to get up or at least move my hands or adjust in the chair. It’s mild, I guess, but it’s there enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one, because I don’t really think of myself as having a big problem with organizing or time management. I mean, I’ve had normal moments where I underestimate how long something will take, or I’ll have to remind myself what step comes next, but I don’t know if that’s more than what most people deal with.\n\nAt work, I usually just try to go by whatever the process is or whatever needs to be handled next, so there’s some structure already there. If you mean like planning out a whole day from scratch or keeping track of a bunch of separate things, I guess I can get a little scattered sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a major issue.\n\nSo… maybe mildly at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I “often” have trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anger, excitement, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” at least not in a way that feels out of the ordinary to me. I mean, I definitely get frustrated sometimes — like anybody does — especially if something is dragging on or if I feel like I’m not getting what I need from a situation. But I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly having these big emotional swings or blowing up.\n\nWith anger, I usually try to keep it contained. I might feel irritated inside, and maybe I get quieter or a little short, but I don’t think I’m regularly losing my temper. Excitement… I mean, sure, I can get excited about things, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or hard to control.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I have emotions, obviously, and I can get frustrated, but I haven’t really noticed strong emotions being a major problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in line or for your turn in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that some, especially in work situations. Like in a team huddle, if I’m waiting for my turn to speak or waiting for them to get around to whatever part involves me, I can get kind of antsy. I’ll be standing there or sitting there trying to look like I’m just listening normally, but inside I’m sort of waiting, waiting, waiting, and I start wanting to jump in before it’s really my turn.\n\nSame thing with the help queue at work. If I’m waiting for the next thing to come through, or waiting for something to be assigned, I catch myself checking the screen over and over. Like I know it’s not going to make it move faster, but I keep looking anyway. It’s not like I’m making a scene or anything, but I can feel myself getting impatient and wanting to just do something or get to my part already.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say I have some trouble with waiting my turn, but it’s more like I get restless and impatient internally than anything dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do that sometimes. I don’t usually mean it as interrupting, exactly, but I can see how it probably comes across that way.\n\nAt work, if I’m near coworkers and I hear them talking through a customer issue — like they’re trying to figure out what to tell someone or where to find something in the system — I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’re done explaining it, or before anyone actually asked me. In my head it’s like, “Oh, I know what that is,” and I just say it because I’m trying to help and move things along. But then afterward I realize, okay, maybe they were still working through it, or maybe the person wasn’t talking to me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to take over or be rude. It’s more like the answer pops up and I blurt it out a little too quickly. I try to catch myself, but yeah, I’d say that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished speaking in social or group settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen, mostly at work. I don’t think I’m doing it in a social way where I’m trying to dominate the conversation or anything like that, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll kind of jump in with the answer before they’ve finished talking.\n\nLike, someone might be explaining what a customer is asking about, or another coworker is trying to figure out where something is in the system, and before they even get all the way through it I’ll say, “Oh, you need to do this,” or “That’s over here,” or whatever. Sometimes nobody actually asked me — I just hear enough to think I know the answer, and it comes out.\n\nI don’t mean to be rude. In my mind I’m being helpful and saving time, especially if I’ve dealt with the same issue before. But I can tell there are times where I probably should’ve waited and let them finish, because I’m jumping in a little too soon. So yes, I’d say I do blurt things out like that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble controlling your impulses in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m out of control or anything dramatic, but in conversation at work, I do have trouble stopping myself from jumping in sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk and I hear a couple coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll catch enough of what they’re saying and my brain kind of goes, “Oh, I know the answer.” And then before I really think about whether they asked me, or whether they’re even done explaining the issue, I’ll just say it. I’ll be like, “No, you have to go to this screen,” or “That customer needs this form,” or whatever it is.\n\nI don’t mean it in a rude way. In my head I’m trying to help and maybe save them time, especially if it’s something I’ve dealt with before. But I can tell afterward that maybe I cut into their conversation, or I answered before they were finished talking. Sometimes I’ll realize, “Okay, I probably should’ve waited another few seconds and let them get through it.”\n\nSo yeah, that’s probably the main way it shows up socially for me — more at work than anywhere else. It’s like the answer comes to me and I have a hard time holding it back long enough to see if it’s actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it was there even when I was a kid. I didn’t really think of it that way at the time, of course — I was just “not applying myself” or “rushing” or whatever they called it back then.\n\nIn grade school, I remember teachers writing comments like I was bright, but careless. That was a pretty common theme. Like I understood the work, but I’d make mistakes because I didn’t stay with it carefully, or I’d turn something in that looked rushed. And I know I got reminded to stay on task — stay with my work, stop drifting off, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd the talking part too… I had teachers say I talked out of turn. Not like I was trying to cause trouble, but I’d speak up when I wasn’t supposed to, or answer before I was called on. So yes, I’d say I did have trouble with paying attention and sitting still in the sense that the teachers were noticing it pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms interfered with your relationships or work performance in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say at work, yes. Not so much with customers directly — I usually get along with customers pretty well, and I try hard to be helpful — but it has started showing up more in my actual job performance.\n\nLike in reviews, my supervisors have pointed out that I’ll have unfinished tickets, or I’ll miss something in a ticket and then it creates extra work later. Sometimes it’s not a huge mistake by itself, but it slows the team down because somebody else has to go back and fix it, or follow up, or ask me what happened with it. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care or I’m being lazy.\n\nI think that’s the part that bothers me the most — I feel like I’m working hard, and I’m not trying to make things harder for anybody, but then I’ll get feedback like, “You left this open,” or “This wasn’t completed all the way,” or “This had the wrong information,” and I can see how it affects the workflow. So I wouldn’t say it’s ruined my work or anything, but it’s significant enough that supervisors are bringing it up now.\n\nRelationships… I mean, generally I get along with people okay. I don’t think I’m having major relationship problems from it. But work-wise, yes, it’s definitely causing some problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you often run, climb, or play vigorously as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember the running and climbing part specifically, like whether I was more than other kids on the playground. What I do recognize, though, is that feeling of not wanting to be stuck still.\n\nEven now, if I’m sitting through something slow, like a training video at work or a long appointment where I’m just supposed to sit and listen, I start feeling kind of boxed in. It’s not panic or anything like that, but I get this strong need to stand up, stretch, shift around, or at least do something with my hands. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I need to move a little or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.”\n\nSo as a kid, I’m sure I was active, but I can’t honestly give you a clear memory of “I was always climbing on things.” The part I can speak to is that sitting still for slow stuff has always been hard in that restless kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Did you often engage in physical activities like running or playing sports as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I ran around and played like kids do, but I don’t have a clear memory of being especially into sports or constantly running more than anyone else.\n\nI wasn’t, like, the kid with a whole list of organized sports I was serious about, at least not that I remember. And I can’t really say, “Yes, I was always doing physical activities,” because I don’t want to overstate it. I remember more of the school comments and the classroom stuff than I remember whether I was unusually active outside.\n\nSo… maybe some normal running around and playing, but I don’t know that I can say it was often or excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in a major way. I mean, I’m sure people close to me have probably gotten annoyed with me at times — everybody does things that irritate people — but I can’t point to, like, a relationship that fell apart because of this, or some big ongoing conflict that I’d say is clearly from my symptoms.\n\nIt’s easier for me to see the work side, because there are reviews and tickets and specific things people can point to. In my personal life it’s a little fuzzier. I generally get along with people okay, and I don’t feel like I’m constantly fighting with family or friends because of it.\n\nSo… I guess my answer would be no, not that I’ve really noticed. Maybe there are little irritations here and there, but nothing I’d call a significant relationship problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to significantly adjust your daily routine or activities because of your difficulty with sitting still or focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly at work. I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely rearranged my whole life around it, but I have had to change how I handle my day because it’s started showing up in my performance reviews.\n\nLike, my supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or tickets where I made mistakes that then slow other people down because someone has to go back and fix it or follow up. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care, and I generally do well with customers — I’m polite, I try to be helpful, I don’t think people complain about how I treat them. But the follow-through and keeping track of everything can get away from me.\n\nSo I’ve tried to adjust by being more deliberate, like checking things more than once, trying to keep notes, trying to slow myself down before closing something out. But even with that, it’s been enough that supervisors are bringing it up, so I guess that counts as me having to adjust my routine. It’s not just an annoyance in my head anymore; it’s affecting the work and the team a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I think so — at least the fidgeting part. I’m not sure I would’ve called it “excessive” at the time, because when you’re a kid you don’t really think of it that way, but I do remember being the kind of person who couldn’t quite keep still.

Even now, if I’m trying to sit calmly and look like I’m paying attention, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen without even realizing I’m doing it. Someone might point it out, or I’ll suddenly notice my leg has been going the whole time. In meetings especially, I can look pretty still from the waist up, but underneath the table my foot is moving constantly.

So going back to childhood, yes, I’d say there was probably a lot of that kind of restlessness. Not necessarily running around wildly all the time, but fidgeting, shifting, moving my hands or feet — that sounds familiar.

Maybe there are little irritations here and there, but nothing I’d call a significant relationship problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to significantly adjust your daily routine or activities because of your difficulty with sitting still or focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly at work. I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely rearranged my whole life around it, but I have had to change how I handle my day because it’s started showing up in my performance reviews.\n\nLike, my supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets, or tickets where I made mistakes that then slow other people down because someone has to go back and fix it or follow up. And that’s frustrating, because I really am trying. It’s not like I don’t care, and I generally do well with customers — I’m polite, I try to be helpful, I don’t think people complain about how I treat them. But the follow-through and keeping track of everything can get away from me.\n\nSo I’ve tried to adjust by being more deliberate, like checking things more than once, trying to keep notes, trying to slow myself down before closing something out. But even with that, it’s been enough that supervisors are bringing it up, so I guess that counts as me having to adjust my routine. It’s not just an annoyance in my head anymore; it’s affecting the work and the team a bit.\n\nDoctor: Did you often fidget or exhibit excessive restlessness as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — at least the fidgeting part. I’m not sure I would’ve called it “excessive” at the time, because when you’re a kid you don’t really think of it that way, but I do remember being the kind of person who couldn’t quite keep still.\n\nEven now, if I’m trying to sit calmly and look like I’m paying attention, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen without even realizing I’m doing it. Someone might point it out, or I’ll suddenly notice my leg has been going the whole time. In meetings especially, I can look pretty still from the waist up, but underneath the table my foot is moving constantly.\n\nSo going back to childhood, yes, I’d say there was probably a lot of that kind of restlessness. Not necessarily running around wildly all the time, but fidgeting, shifting, moving my hands or feet — that sounds familiar.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely by high school, although honestly when I look back, it was there even before that. It’s one of those things I didn’t really understand at the time — I just thought I was kind of scattered, or that teachers were being picky.

But even in grade school, I remember comments coming home that were basically like, “He’s bright, but careless,” or that I talked out of turn, or needed reminders to stay with the work. That phrase “bright but careless” kind of stuck with me, because it showed up more than once in different ways. Like I could understand the material, but I’d miss details, rush through something, forget part of the assignment, or drift off unless someone redirected me.

So yes, in high school I still had trouble with focus and sitting still, but it wasn’t like it suddenly started there. It was more that it had been part of how I functioned for a long time, and school was where adults were noticing it and writing it down.
